You manage a team, you hold everything together, and then you’re standing in the kitchen doing this.” It feels like I’ve completely blown the version of myself I try so hard to be during the day — controlled, competent, put together.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about what I should have done instead. Like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth and gone to bed like a normal person. I replay it in my head, almost like if I shame myself enough I’ll prevent it next time, but of course that doesn’t really help. I mostly just end up sitting there feeling awful about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you are eating at night, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished?\n\nPatient: It’s more after I’ve finished, I think. I mean, there can be this little voice during it saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt — the heavy, horrible part — lands afterward, when I sort of come back to myself and see what’s there.\n\nThat’s when I feel embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I’ll look at the dishes or containers and think, “How did you let this happen again?” It feels like I’ve blown up this disciplined, capable person I spend all day trying to be. And then my mind goes straight to all the things I should have done instead — like I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or gone upstairs before it got to that point. I just sit there feeling like I failed myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating when these nighttime episodes start?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t. I mean, at the beginning I can convince myself that I’m making a choice — like I’m just going to have a little something, or I’ll stop after one thing — but once it starts, especially if it’s after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like something switches off.\n\nThe part of me that normally weighs things out and says, “Okay, Patricia, enough, go upstairs,” just kind of goes offline. And I’m still there mentally, if that makes sense. I’m not unaware. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, this is enough, you don’t need more,” but my body is still moving around the kitchen, still reaching for things, still opening something else. So it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly deciding what to eat. It feels more like I’m being pulled along by it, even while another part of me is trying to slam on the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly, actually. That’s one of the things that bothers me when I think back on it.\n\nI’m usually standing at the kitchen island, not even sitting down like it’s a real meal, and I’ll just be… eating while I’m scrolling through work emails or half-looking at my phone. And it’s fast. Like, I’m barely chewing properly sometimes, or I’m already reaching for the next bite before I’ve really tasted the one I just had.\n\nThere are times where I’ll look down and the container is just empty, and I have this moment of, “Wait, when did that happen?” Not like I blacked out, but more like I wasn’t really registering it. I couldn’t tell you if it tasted good after the first few bites. I just know I kept eating, and then suddenly it was gone. That part feels awful, because it makes me feel like I wasn’t even enjoying it — I was just shoveling it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before these nighttime eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those out, honestly. I mean… I don’t always stop and think, “I’m anxious right now,” right before it happens. Sometimes it’s only afterward that I look back and think, okay, maybe I was wound up or unsettled.\n\nThere are definitely evenings where I feel tense or kind of emotionally stirred up before I end up in the kitchen, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always a clear feeling of anxiety or stress in the moment. Sometimes it’s more like this restless, uncomfortable feeling — like I can’t settle down, but I’m not naming it as anything. Other times I’m just tired and sort of moving through the evening on autopilot, and then suddenly I’m eating.\n\nSo… I think there’s often something going on beforehand, but I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to say exactly what it is every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control the amount of food you eat once you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That’s probably the most upsetting part of it, because I can tell myself beforehand, “Just have a small amount,” or “Just take one thing and then go to bed,” but once I actually start, it doesn’t feel like I can manage the amount in any normal way.\n\nEspecially on nights when things have been tense with my husband, it’s like the part of me that usually makes decisions just shuts down. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then my hand is still reaching for something else, or I’m opening another package, or going back for more even though I already know I’m going to feel terrible about it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there calmly choosing a portion and being okay with it. It feels more like once the eating starts, the stopping point disappears. I know there should be a stopping point, but I can’t seem to make myself follow it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or anxious?\n\nPatient: I think maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I can say that clearly. Like, I don’t always have this obvious feeling of “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.” It’s not that neat in my head.\n\nThere are nights where, looking back, I can see I probably was emotional — tense, or bothered, or just kind of unsettled — and then the eating happened. I’ll be in the kitchen and then I’m eating, and only afterward I start trying to piece together what I was feeling before.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, and I’m embarrassed even saying that. It’s mostly at night, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the kitchen and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, like something normal, and then somehow it turns into… a lot. Like, I’ll realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, then the dessert I brought home from a client dinner, and then whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting.\n\nIt feels awful to admit, because during the day I can be very put together — I’m a marketing manager, I’m used to managing people and details and presentations — but then at night it’s like I’m standing there with containers open and wrappers around, and I suddenly see how much I’ve eaten. That’s why I came in. I don’t feel like I have a good handle on it anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you feel like you can stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I can, and I’ll even sort of make a bargain in my head, like, “Okay, Patricia, just this one plate,” or “Just finish this and then go upstairs.” But once I’ve started, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband or I’m already wound up, it feels like the part of me that makes sensible decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’m aware of it while it’s happening, which is the worst part. I’ll be thinking, “Stop. This is enough. You’re not even enjoying it anymore.” But my hand is still opening another container or reaching back into the pantry. It’s like I’m watching myself continue, and I can’t seem to interrupt it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these nighttime eating episodes, once you've realized what you've done?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… honestly, embarrassed. Deeply embarrassed. Not just like, “Oh, I ate too much,” but like I’ve done something shameful and weak, which I know sounds harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nI’ll look at the containers or the wrappers and feel disgusted with myself, like, “What is wrong with you? You’re a grown woman. You’re supposed to be disciplined. You manage a team, you hold everything together, and then you’re standing in the kitchen doing this.” It feels like I’ve completely blown the version of myself I try so hard to be during the day — controlled, competent, put together.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about what I should have done instead. Like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth and gone to bed like a normal person. I replay it in my head, almost like if I shame myself enough I’ll prevent it next time, but of course that doesn’t really help. I mostly just end up sitting there feeling awful about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you are eating at night, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished?\n\nPatient: It’s more after I’ve finished, I think. I mean, there can be this little voice during it saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt — the heavy, horrible part — lands afterward, when I sort of come back to myself and see what’s there.\n\nThat’s when I feel embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I’ll look at the dishes or containers and think, “How did you let this happen again?” It feels like I’ve blown up this disciplined, capable person I spend all day trying to be. And then my mind goes straight to all the things I should have done instead — like I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or gone upstairs before it got to that point. I just sit there feeling like I failed myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating when these nighttime episodes start?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t. I mean, at the beginning I can convince myself that I’m making a choice — like I’m just going to have a little something, or I’ll stop after one thing — but once it starts, especially if it’s after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like something switches off.\n\nThe part of me that normally weighs things out and says, “Okay, Patricia, enough, go upstairs,” just kind of goes offline. And I’m still there mentally, if that makes sense. I’m not unaware. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, this is enough, you don’t need more,” but my body is still moving around the kitchen, still reaching for things, still opening something else. So it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly deciding what to eat. It feels more like I’m being pulled along by it, even while another part of me is trying to slam on the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly, actually. That’s one of the things that bothers me when I think back on it.\n\nI’m usually standing at the kitchen island, not even sitting down like it’s a real meal, and I’ll just be… eating while I’m scrolling through work emails or half-looking at my phone. And it’s fast. Like, I’m barely chewing properly sometimes, or I’m already reaching for the next bite before I’ve really tasted the one I just had.\n\nThere are times where I’ll look down and the container is just empty, and I have this moment of, “Wait, when did that happen?” Not like I blacked out, but more like I wasn’t really registering it. I couldn’t tell you if it tasted good after the first few bites. I just know I kept eating, and then suddenly it was gone. That part feels awful, because it makes me feel like I wasn’t even enjoying it — I was just shoveling it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before these nighttime eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those out, honestly. I mean… I don’t always stop and think, “I’m anxious right now,” right before it happens. Sometimes it’s only afterward that I look back and think, okay, maybe I was wound up or unsettled.\n\nThere are definitely evenings where I feel tense or kind of emotionally stirred up before I end up in the kitchen, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always a clear feeling of anxiety or stress in the moment. Sometimes it’s more like this restless, uncomfortable feeling — like I can’t settle down, but I’m not naming it as anything. Other times I’m just tired and sort of moving through the evening on autopilot, and then suddenly I’m eating.\n\nSo… I think there’s often something going on beforehand, but I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to say exactly what it is every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control the amount of food you eat once you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That’s probably the most upsetting part of it, because I can tell myself beforehand, “Just have a small amount,” or “Just take one thing and then go to bed,” but once I actually start, it doesn’t feel like I can manage the amount in any normal way.\n\nEspecially on nights when things have been tense with my husband, it’s like the part of me that usually makes decisions just shuts down. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then my hand is still reaching for something else, or I’m opening another package, or going back for more even though I already know I’m going to feel terrible about it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there calmly choosing a portion and being okay with it. It feels more like once the eating starts, the stopping point disappears. I know there should be a stopping point, but I can’t seem to make myself follow it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or anxious?\n\nPatient: I think maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I can say that clearly. Like, I don’t always have this obvious feeling of “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.” It’s not that neat in my head.\n\nThere are nights where, looking back, I can see I probably was emotional — tense, or bothered, or just kind of unsettled — and then the eating happened. But in the actual moment, it often feels less like I’m making that connection and more like I’m just… drifting into it. I’ll be in the kitchen and then I’m eating, and only afterward I start trying to piece together what I was feeling before.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. It might be connected to emotions sometimes, but I haven’t really been able to pin down whether that’s the reason every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, yes, that’s exactly what it feels like, and it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making excuses, because I know technically I’m the one eating. But in those moments, once I’ve started, it really does feel like the decision-making part of me kind of goes offline.\n\nEspecially if it’s been a tense night with my husband, or there’s been that uncomfortable feeling in the house, I’ll start eating and then it’s like I can’t get the brakes to work. I can be thinking very clearly, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more.” And I’ll believe that. I’ll want to stop. But then I’m still reaching for something else, or taking another bite, or going back again.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s part of why it feels so awful. It’s like there’s this split between what I’m thinking and what I’m actually doing, and the thinking part just isn’t strong enough in that moment to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating at night is usually a response to feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I really don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nSometimes I can look back afterward and think, okay, maybe I was tense, or irritated, or upset in a way I wasn’t really admitting to myself. Like there’s this background feeling that something is wrong, and then later the eating happens. But in the moment, I don’t always experience it as, “I’m emotional, so I’m going to eat.” It’s not that direct for me.\n\nA lot of the time it feels almost automatic, or like I don’t notice what led up to it until after it’s already happened. Then I’m sitting there trying to reconstruct it, like, “Was I anxious? Was I upset? Was I just tired?” And I honestly don’t always know.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, yes, but I can’t say it’s usually that, because I haven’t been able to track it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your actions while you're eating at night?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’m physically doing it, so part of me wants to say I must be in control, because it’s my hand reaching for the food and I’m the one standing there in the kitchen. But it doesn’t feel like control the way it should feel.\n\nOnce I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband where I already feel kind of wound up or unsettled, it’s like something switches off. The decision-making part of me just sort of goes offline. I can be completely aware of what’s happening and still not be able to stop it.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more.” And I mean it when I think it. It’s not like I’m enjoying it in some carefree way and deciding to keep going. I’m thinking I should stop, I want to stop, I’m telling myself to stop — and then I’m still reaching for more. Or I’ll finish something and think, okay, that’s it, and then a minute later I’m opening something else.\n\nSo no, in those moments I don’t feel fully in control. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though the part of me that knows better is trying to get me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly, partly because I know it can make me feel worse, so I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI’ve had moments where my clothes feel a little different, or I wonder if I’ve gained, but I don’t know if that’s an actual significant change or just me being very aware of my body and worrying about it. I haven’t been tracking it in any organized way. So I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure that I’ve noticed anything major recently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

the mornings after these nights I feel drained and ashamed, and I’ve started skipping plans like yoga or creative time because I don’t want to face anyone.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n Female / 40 / marketing_manager / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your eating at night is usually a response to feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I really don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nSometimes I can look back afterward and think, okay, maybe I was tense, or irritated, or upset in a way I wasn’t really admitting to myself. Like there’s this background feeling that something is wrong, and then later the eating happens. But in the moment, I don’t always experience it as, “I’m emotional, so I’m going to eat.” It’s not that direct for me.\n\nA lot of the time it feels almost automatic, or like I don’t notice what led up to it until after it’s already happened. Then I’m sitting there trying to reconstruct it, like, “Was I anxious? Was I upset? Was I just tired?” And I honestly don’t always know.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, yes, but I can’t say it’s usually that, because I haven’t been able to track it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your actions while you're eating at night?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’m physically doing it, so part of me wants to say I must be in control, because it’s my hand reaching for the food and I’m the one standing there in the kitchen. But it doesn’t feel like control the way it should feel.\n\nOnce I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband where I already feel kind of wound up or unsettled, it’s like something switches off. The decision-making part of me just sort of goes offline. I can be completely aware of what’s happening and still not be able to stop it.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more.” And I mean it when I think it. It’s not like I’m enjoying it in some carefree way and deciding to keep going. I’m thinking I should stop, I want to stop, I’m telling myself to stop — and then I’m still reaching for more. Or I’ll finish something and think, okay, that’s it, and then a minute later I’m opening something else.\n\nSo no, in those moments I don’t feel fully in control. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though the part of me that knows better is trying to get me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly, partly because I know it can make me feel worse, so I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI’ve had moments where my clothes feel a little different, or I wonder if I’ve gained, but I don’t know if that’s an actual significant change or just me being very aware of my body and worrying about it. I haven’t been tracking it in any organized way. So I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure that I’ve noticed anything major recently.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating at night, as opposed to just after you've finished?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. I mean, afterward is definitely when it becomes clearer to me — that’s when I can sit there and think, “Why did I do that?” or start replaying it in my head.

During it, though… sometimes there’s a running commentary, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. It’s more like this uncomfortable awareness that I probably shouldn’t be doing it, or that I’m going to feel bad about it. I might think, “This isn’t a good idea,” but at the same time I’m still eating, so it’s kind of muddled.

I don’t know if that counts as being self-critical while it’s happening, because it’s not always a clear, harsh thought in the moment. A lot of the sharper self-criticism seems to come after I’ve stopped and I’m more fully aware of what happened. During it, it’s more like… I know, but I’m not fully letting myself know, if that makes sense.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, and I’m embarrassed even saying that. It’s mostly at night, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the kitchen and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, like something normal, and then somehow it turns into… a lot. Like, I’ll realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, then the dessert I brought home from a client dinner, and then whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting.\n\nIt feels awful to admit, because during the day I can be very put together — I’m a marketing manager, I’m used to managing people and details and presentations — but then at night it’s like I’m standing there with containers open and wrappers around, and I suddenly see how much I’ve eaten. That’s why I came in. I don’t feel like I have a good handle on it anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you feel like you can stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I can, and I’ll even sort of make a bargain in my head, like, “Okay, Patricia, just this one plate,” or “Just finish this and then go upstairs.” But once I’ve started, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband or I’m already wound up, it feels like the part of me that makes sensible decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’m aware of it while it’s happening, which is the worst part. I’ll be thinking, “Stop. This is enough. You’re not even enjoying it anymore.” But my hand is still opening another container or reaching back into the pantry. It’s like I’m watching myself continue, and I can’t seem to interrupt it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these nighttime eating episodes, once you've realized what you've done?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… honestly, embarrassed. Deeply embarrassed. Not just like, “Oh, I ate too much,” but like I’ve done something shameful and weak, which I know sounds harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nI’ll look at the containers or the wrappers and feel disgusted with myself, like, “What is wrong with you? You’re a grown woman. You’re supposed to be disciplined. You manage a team, you hold everything together, and then you’re standing in the kitchen doing this.” It feels like I’ve completely blown the version of myself I try so hard to be during the day — controlled, competent, put together.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about what I should have done instead. Like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth and gone to bed like a normal person. I replay it in my head, almost like if I shame myself enough I’ll prevent it next time, but of course that doesn’t really help. I mostly just end up sitting there feeling awful about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you are eating at night, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished?\n\nPatient: It’s more after I’ve finished, I think. I mean, there can be this little voice during it saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt — the heavy, horrible part — lands afterward, when I sort of come back to myself and see what’s there.\n\nThat’s when I feel embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I’ll look at the dishes or containers and think, “How did you let this happen again?” It feels like I’ve blown up this disciplined, capable person I spend all day trying to be. And then my mind goes straight to all the things I should have done instead — like I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or gone upstairs before it got to that point. I just sit there feeling like I failed myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating when these nighttime episodes start?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t. I mean, at the beginning I can convince myself that I’m making a choice — like I’m just going to have a little something, or I’ll stop after one thing — but once it starts, especially if it’s after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like something switches off.\n\nThe part of me that normally weighs things out and says, “Okay, Patricia, enough, go upstairs,” just kind of goes offline. And I’m still there mentally, if that makes sense. I’m not unaware. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, this is enough, you don’t need more,” but my body is still moving around the kitchen, still reaching for things, still opening something else. So it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly deciding what to eat. It feels more like I’m being pulled along by it, even while another part of me is trying to slam on the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly, actually. That’s one of the things that bothers me when I think back on it.\n\nI’m usually standing at the kitchen island, not even sitting down like it’s a real meal, and I’ll just be… eating while I’m scrolling through work emails or half-looking at my phone. And it’s fast. Like, I’m barely chewing properly sometimes, or I’m already reaching for the next bite before I’ve really tasted the one I just had.\n\nThere are times where I’ll look down and the container is just empty, and I have this moment of, “Wait, when did that happen?” Not like I blacked out, but more like I wasn’t really registering it. I couldn’t tell you if it tasted good after the first few bites. I just know I kept eating, and then suddenly it was gone. That part feels awful, because it makes me feel like I wasn’t even enjoying it — I was just shoveling it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before these nighttime eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those out, honestly. I mean… I don’t always stop and think, “I’m anxious right now,” right before it happens. Sometimes it’s only afterward that I look back and think, okay, maybe I was wound up or unsettled.\n\nThere are definitely evenings where I feel tense or kind of emotionally stirred up before I end up in the kitchen, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always a clear feeling of anxiety or stress in the moment. Sometimes it’s more like this restless, uncomfortable feeling — like I can’t settle down, but I’m not naming it as anything. Other times I’m just tired and sort of moving through the evening on autopilot, and then suddenly I’m eating.\n\nSo… I think there’s often something going on beforehand, but I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to say exactly what it is every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control the amount of food you eat once you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That’s probably the most upsetting part of it, because I can tell myself beforehand, “Just have a small amount,” or “Just take one thing and then go to bed,” but once I actually start, it doesn’t feel like I can manage the amount in any normal way.\n\nEspecially on nights when things have been tense with my husband, it’s like the part of me that usually makes decisions just shuts down. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then my hand is still reaching for something else, or I’m opening another package, or going back for more even though I already know I’m going to feel terrible about it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there calmly choosing a portion and being okay with it. It feels more like once the eating starts, the stopping point disappears. I know there should be a stopping point, but I can’t seem to make myself follow it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or anxious?\n\nPatient: I think maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I can say that clearly. Like, I don’t always have this obvious feeling of “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.” It’s not that neat in my head.\n\nThere are nights where, looking back, I can see I probably was emotional — tense, or bothered, or just kind of unsettled — and then the eating happened. But in the actual moment, it often feels less like I’m making that connection and more like I’m just… drifting into it. I’ll be in the kitchen and then I’m eating, and only afterward I start trying to piece together what I was feeling before.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. It might be connected to emotions sometimes, but I haven’t really been able to pin down whether that’s the reason every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, yes, that’s exactly what it feels like, and it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making excuses, because I know technically I’m the one eating. But in those moments, once I’ve started, it really does feel like the decision-making part of me kind of goes offline.\n\nEspecially if it’s been a tense night with my husband, or there’s been that uncomfortable feeling in the house, I’ll start eating and then it’s like I can’t get the brakes to work. I can be thinking very clearly, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more.” And I’ll believe that. I’ll want to stop. But then I’m still reaching for something else, or taking another bite, or going back again.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s part of why it feels so awful. It’s like there’s this split between what I’m thinking and what I’m actually doing, and the thinking part just isn’t strong enough in that moment to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating at night is usually a response to feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I really don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nSometimes I can look back afterward and think, okay, maybe I was tense, or irritated, or upset in a way I wasn’t really admitting to myself. Like there’s this background feeling that something is wrong, and then later the eating happens. But in the moment, I don’t always experience it as, “I’m emotional, so I’m going to eat.” It’s not that direct for me.\n\nA lot of the time it feels almost automatic, or like I don’t notice what led up to it until after it’s already happened. Then I’m sitting there trying to reconstruct it, like, “Was I anxious? Was I upset? Was I just tired?” And I honestly don’t always know.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, yes, but I can’t say it’s usually that, because I haven’t been able to track it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your actions while you're eating at night?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. The decision-making part of me just sort of goes offline. I can be completely aware of what’s happening and still not be able to stop it.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more.” And I mean it when I think it. It’s not like I’m enjoying it in some carefree way and deciding to keep going. I just sit there feeling like I failed myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating when these nighttime episodes start?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t. I mean, at the beginning I can convince myself that I’m making a choice — like I’m just going to have a little something, or I’ll stop after one thing — but once it starts, especially if it’s after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like something switches off.\n\nThe part of me that normally weighs things out and says, “Okay, Patricia, enough, go upstairs,” just kind of goes offline. And I’m still there mentally, if that makes sense. I’m not unaware. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, this is enough, you don’t need more,” but my body is still moving around the kitchen, still reaching for things, still opening something else. So it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly deciding what to eat. It feels more like I’m being pulled along by it, even while another part of me is trying to slam on the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly, actually. That’s one of the things that bothers me when I think back on it.\n\nI’m usually standing at the kitchen island, not even sitting down like it’s a real meal, and I’ll just be… eating while I’m scrolling through work emails or half-looking at my phone. And it’s fast. Like, I’m barely chewing properly sometimes, or I’m already reaching for the next bite before I’ve really tasted the one I just had.\n\nThere are times where I’ll look down and the container is just empty, and I have this moment of, “Wait, when did that happen?” Not like I blacked out, but more like I wasn’t really registering it. I couldn’t tell you if it tasted good after the first few bites. I just know I kept eating, and then suddenly it was gone. That part feels awful, because it makes me feel like I wasn’t even enjoying it — I was just shoveling it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before these nighttime eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those out, honestly. I mean… I don’t always stop and think, “I’m anxious right now,” right before it happens. Sometimes it’s only afterward that I look back and think, okay, maybe I was wound up or unsettled.\n\nThere are definitely evenings where I feel tense or kind of emotionally stirred up before I end up in the kitchen, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always a clear feeling of anxiety or stress in the moment. Sometimes it’s more like this restless, uncomfortable feeling — like I can’t settle down, but I’m not naming it as anything. Other times I’m just tired and sort of moving through the evening on autopilot, and then suddenly I’m eating.\n\nSo… I think there’s often something going on beforehand, but I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to say exactly what it is every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control the amount of food you eat once you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That’s probably the most upsetting part of it, because I can tell myself beforehand, “Just have a small amount,” or “Just take one thing and then go to bed,” but once I actually start, it doesn’t feel like I can manage the amount in any normal way.\n\nEspecially on nights when things have been tense with my husband, it’s like the part of me that usually makes decisions just shuts down. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then my hand is still reaching for something else, or I’m opening another package, or going back for more even though I already know I’m going to feel terrible about it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there calmly choosing a portion and being okay with it. It feels more like once the eating starts, the stopping point disappears. I know there should be a stopping point, but I can’t seem to make myself follow it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or anxious?\n\nPatient: I think maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I can say that clearly. Like, I don’t always have this obvious feeling of “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.” It’s not that neat in my head.\n\nThere are nights where, looking back, I can see I probably was emotional — tense, or bothered, or just kind of unsettled — and then the eating happened. But in the actual moment, it often feels less like I’m making that connection and more like I’m just… drifting into it. I’ll be in the kitchen and then I’m eating, and only afterward I start trying to piece together what I was feeling before.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. It might be connected to emotions sometimes, but I haven’t really been able to pin down whether that’s the reason every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, yes, that’s exactly what it feels like, and it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making excuses, because I know technically I’m the one eating. But in those moments, once I’ve started, it really does feel like the decision-making part of me kind of goes offline.\n\nEspecially if it’s been a tense night with my husband, or there’s been that uncomfortable feeling in the house, I’ll start eating and then it’s like I can’t get the brakes to work. I can be thinking very clearly, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more.” And I’ll believe that. I’ll want to stop. But then I’m still reaching for something else, or taking another bite, or going back again.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s part of why it feels so awful. It’s like there’s this split between what I’m thinking and what I’m actually doing, and the thinking part just isn’t strong enough in that moment to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating at night is usually a response to feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I really don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nSometimes I can look back afterward and think, okay, maybe I was tense, or irritated, or upset in a way I wasn’t really admitting to myself. Like there’s this background feeling that something is wrong, and then later the eating happens. But in the moment, I don’t always experience it as, “I’m emotional, so I’m going to eat.” It’s not that direct for me.\n\nA lot of the time it feels almost automatic, or like I don’t notice what led up to it until after it’s already happened. Then I’m sitting there trying to reconstruct it, like, “Was I anxious? Was I upset? Was I just tired?” And I honestly don’t always know.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, yes, but I can’t say it’s usually that, because I haven’t been able to track it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your actions while you're eating at night?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’m physically doing it, so part of me wants to say I must be in control, because it’s my hand reaching for the food and I’m the one standing there in the kitchen. But it doesn’t feel like control the way it should feel.\n\nOnce I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband where I already feel kind of wound up or unsettled, it’s like something switches off. The decision-making part of me just sort of goes offline. I can be completely aware of what’s happening and still not be able to stop it.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more.” And I mean it when I think it. It’s not like I’m enjoying it in some carefree way and deciding to keep going. I’m thinking I should stop, I want to stop, I’m telling myself to stop — and then I’m still reaching for more. Or I’ll finish something and think, okay, that’s it, and then a minute later I’m opening something else.\n\nSo no, in those moments I don’t feel fully in control. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though the part of me that knows better is trying to get me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly, partly because I know it can make me feel worse, so I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI’ve had moments where my clothes feel a little different, or I wonder if I’ve gained, but I don’t know if that’s an actual significant change or just me being very aware of my body and worrying about it. I haven’t been tracking it in any organized way. So I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure that I’ve noticed anything major recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating at night, as opposed to just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. I mean, afterward is definitely when it becomes clearer to me — that’s when I can sit there and think, “Why did I do that?” or start replaying it in my head.\n\nDuring it, though… sometimes there’s a running commentary, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. It’s more like this uncomfortable awareness that I probably shouldn’t be doing it, or that I’m going to feel bad about it. I might think, “This isn’t a good idea,” but at the same time I’m still eating, so it’s kind of muddled.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as being self-critical while it’s happening, because it’s not always a clear, harsh thought in the moment. A lot of the sharper self-criticism seems to come after I’ve stopped and I’m more fully aware of what happened. During it, it’s more like… I know, but I’m not fully letting myself know, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, it often feels connected to how I’m feeling, but I don’t know if I can say it’s always because I’m emotional in some direct, obvious way.

Sometimes by the time it happens at night, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat,” or “I’m angry, so I need food.” It’s more blurred than that. The day is over, things are quiet, and I’ll realize I feel unsettled or tense or just kind of worn down, but I don’t always name it in the moment. Then I end up eating, and afterward I can look back and wonder whether I was trying to calm myself down or distract myself.

So… yes, maybe there’s an emotional piece, but I don’t know how clearly I recognize it while it’s happening. It’s not like I have a neat pattern written out in my head. It’s more that the eating tends to happen when I’m not feeling quite okay, if that makes sense.

  {
    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, and I’m embarrassed even saying that. It’s mostly at night, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the kitchen and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, like something normal, and then somehow it turns into… a lot. Like, I’ll realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, then the dessert I brought home from a client dinner, and then whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting.\n\nIt feels awful to admit, because during the day I can be very put together — I’m a marketing manager, I’m used to managing people and details and presentations — but then at night it’s like I’m standing there with containers open and wrappers around, and I suddenly see how much I’ve eaten. That’s why I came in. I don’t feel like I have a good handle on it anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you feel like you can stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I can, and I’ll even sort of make a bargain in my head, like, “Okay, Patricia, just this one plate,” or “Just finish this and then go upstairs.” But once I’ve started, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband or I’m already wound up, it feels like the part of me that makes sensible decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’m aware of it while it’s happening, which is the worst part. I’ll be thinking, “Stop. This is enough. You’re not even enjoying it anymore.” But my hand is still opening another container or reaching back into the pantry. It’s like I’m watching myself continue, and I can’t seem to interrupt it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these nighttime eating episodes, once you've realized what you've done?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… honestly, embarrassed. Deeply embarrassed. Not just like, “Oh, I ate too much,” but like I’ve done something shameful and weak, which I know sounds harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nI’ll look at the containers or the wrappers and feel disgusted with myself, like, “What is wrong with you? You’re a grown woman. You’re supposed to be disciplined. You manage a team, you hold everything together, and then you’re standing in the kitchen doing this.” It feels like I’ve completely blown the version of myself I try so hard to be during the day — controlled, competent, put together.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about what I should have done instead. Like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth and gone to bed like a normal person. I replay it in my head, almost like if I shame myself enough I’ll prevent it next time, but of course that doesn’t really help. I mostly just end up sitting there feeling awful about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you are eating at night, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished?\n\nPatient: It’s more after I’ve finished, I think. I mean, there can be this little voice during it saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt — the heavy, horrible part — lands afterward, when I sort of come back to myself and see what’s there.\n\nThat’s when I feel embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I’ll look at the dishes or containers and think, “How did you let this happen again?” It feels like I’ve blown up this disciplined, capable person I spend all day trying to be. And then my mind goes straight to all the things I should have done instead — like I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or gone upstairs before it got to that point. I just sit there feeling like I failed myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating when these nighttime episodes start?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t. I mean, at the beginning I can convince myself that I’m making a choice — like I’m just going to have a little something, or I’ll stop after one thing — but once it starts, especially if it’s after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like something switches off.\n\nThe part of me that normally weighs things out and says, “Okay, Patricia, enough, go upstairs,” just kind of goes offline. And I’m still there mentally, if that makes sense. I’m not unaware. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, this is enough, you don’t need more,” but my body is still moving around the kitchen, still reaching for things, still opening something else. So it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly deciding what to eat. It feels more like I’m being pulled along by it, even while another part of me is trying to slam on the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly, actually. That’s one of the things that bothers me when I think back on it.\n\nI’m usually standing at the kitchen island, not even sitting down like it’s a real meal, and I’ll just be… eating while I’m scrolling through work emails or half-looking at my phone. And it’s fast. Like, I’m barely chewing properly sometimes, or I’m already reaching for the next bite before I’ve really tasted the one I just had.\n\nThere are times where I’ll look down and the container is just empty, and I have this moment of, “Wait, when did that happen?” Not like I blacked out, but more like I wasn’t really registering it. I couldn’t tell you if it tasted good after the first few bites. I just know I kept eating, and then suddenly it was gone. That part feels awful, because it makes me feel like I wasn’t even enjoying it — I was just shoveling it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before these nighttime eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those out, honestly. I mean… I don’t always stop and think, “I’m anxious right now,” right before it happens. Sometimes it’s only afterward that I look back and think, okay, maybe I was wound up or unsettled.\n\nThere are definitely evenings where I feel tense or kind of emotionally stirred up before I end up in the kitchen, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always a clear feeling of anxiety or stress in the moment. Sometimes it’s more like this restless, uncomfortable feeling — like I can’t settle down, but I’m not naming it as anything. Other times I’m just tired and sort of moving through the evening on autopilot, and then suddenly I’m eating.\n\nSo… I think there’s often something going on beforehand, but I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to say exactly what it is every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control the amount of food you eat once you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That’s probably the most upsetting part of it, because I can tell myself beforehand, “Just have a small amount,” or “Just take one thing and then go to bed,” but once I actually start, it doesn’t feel like I can manage the amount in any normal way.\n\nEspecially on nights when things have been tense with my husband, it’s like the part of me that usually makes decisions just shuts down. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then my hand is still reaching for something else, or I’m opening another package, or going back for more even though I already know I’m going to feel terrible about it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there calmly choosing a portion and being okay with it. It feels more like once the eating starts, the stopping point disappears. I know there should be a stopping point, but I can’t seem to make myself follow it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or anxious?\n\nPatient: I think maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I can say that clearly. Like, I don’t always have this obvious feeling of “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.” It’s not that neat in my head.\n\nThere are nights where, looking back, I can see I probably was emotional — tense, or bothered, or just kind of unsettled — and then the eating happened. But in the actual moment, it often feels less like I’m making that connection and more like I’m just… drifting into it. I’ll be in the kitchen and then I’m eating, and only afterward I start trying to piece together what I was feeling before.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. It might be connected to emotions sometimes, but I haven’t really been able to pin down whether that’s the reason every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, yes, that’s exactly what it feels like, and it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making excuses, because I know technically I’m the one eating. But in those moments, once I’ve started, it really does feel like the decision-making part of me kind of goes offline.\n\nEspecially if it’s been a tense night with my husband, or there’s been that uncomfortable feeling in the house, I’ll start eating and then it’s like I can’t get the brakes to work. I can be thinking very clearly, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more.” And I’ll believe that. I’ll want to stop. But then I’m still reaching for something else, or taking another bite, or going back again.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s part of why it feels so awful. It’s like there’s this split between what I’m thinking and what I’m actually doing, and the thinking part just isn’t strong enough in that moment to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating at night is usually a response to feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I really don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nSometimes I can look back afterward and think, okay, maybe I was tense, or irritated, or upset in a way I wasn’t really admitting to myself. Like there’s this background feeling that something is wrong, and then later the eating happens. But in the moment, I don’t always experience it as, “I’m emotional, so I’m going to eat.” It’s not that direct for me.\n\nA lot of the time it feels almost automatic, or like I don’t notice what led up to it until after it’s already happened. Then I’m sitting there trying to reconstruct it, like, “Was I anxious? Was I upset? Was I just tired?” And I honestly don’t always know.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, yes, but I can’t say it’s usually that, because I haven’t been able to track it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your actions while you're eating at night?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’m physically doing it, so part of me wants to say I must be in control, because it’s my hand reaching for the food and I’m the one standing there in the kitchen. But it doesn’t feel like control the way it should feel.\n\nOnce I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband where I already feel kind of wound up or unsettled, it’s like something switches off. The decision-making part of me just sort of goes offline. I can be completely aware of what’s happening and still not be able to stop it.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more.” And I mean it when I think it. It’s not like I’m enjoying it in some carefree way and deciding to keep going. I’m thinking I should stop, I want to stop, I’m telling myself to stop — and then I’m still reaching for more. Or I’ll finish something and think, okay, that’s it, and then a minute later I’m opening something else.\n\nSo no, in those moments I don’t feel fully in control. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though the part of me that knows better is trying to get me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly, partly because I know it can make me feel worse, so I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI’ve had moments where my clothes feel a little different, or I wonder if I’ve gained, but I don’t know if that’s an actual significant change or just me being very aware of my body and worrying about it. I haven’t been tracking it in any organized way. So I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure that I’ve noticed anything major recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating at night, as opposed to just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. I mean, afterward is definitely when it becomes clearer to me — that’s when I can sit there and think, “Why did I do that?” or start replaying it in my head.\n\nDuring it, though… sometimes there’s a running commentary, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. It’s more like this uncomfortable awareness that I probably shouldn’t be doing it, or that I’m going to feel bad about it. I might think, “This isn’t a good idea,” but at the same time I’m still eating, so it’s kind of muddled.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as being self-critical while it’s happening, because it’s not always a clear, harsh thought in the moment. A lot of the sharper self-criticism seems to come after I’ve stopped and I’m more fully aware of what happened. During it, it’s more like… I know, but I’m not fully letting myself know, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat at night because you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, it often feels connected to how I’m feeling, but I don’t know if I can say it’s always because I’m emotional in some direct, obvious way.\n\nSometimes by the time it happens at night, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat,” or “I’m angry, so I need food.” It’s more blurred than that. The day is over, things are quiet, and I’ll realize I feel unsettled or tense or just kind of worn down, but I don’t always name it in the moment. Then I end up eating, and afterward I can look back and wonder whether I was trying to calm myself down or distract myself.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there’s an emotional piece, but I don’t know how clearly I recognize it while it’s happening. It’s not like I have a neat pattern written out in my head. It’s more that the eating tends to happen when I’m not feeling quite okay, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t weigh myself regularly, partly because I know it can make me feel worse, so I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI’ve had moments where my clothes feel a little different, or I wonder if I’ve gained, but I don’t know if that’s an actual significant change or just me being very aware of my body and worrying about it. I haven’t been tracking it in any organized way. So I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure that I’ve noticed anything major recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating at night, as opposed to just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. I mean, afterward is definitely when it becomes clearer to me — that’s when I can sit there and think, “Why did I do that?” or start replaying it in my head.\n\nDuring it, though… sometimes there’s a running commentary, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. It’s more like this uncomfortable awareness that I probably shouldn’t be doing it, or that I’m going to feel bad about it. I might think, “This isn’t a good idea,” but at the same time I’m still eating, so it’s kind of muddled.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as being self-critical while it’s happening, because it’s not always a clear, harsh thought in the moment. A lot of the sharper self-criticism seems to come after I’ve stopped and I’m more fully aware of what happened. During it, it’s more like… I know, but I’m not fully letting myself know, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat at night because you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, it often feels connected to how I’m feeling, but I don’t know if I can say it’s always because I’m emotional in some direct, obvious way.\n\nSometimes by the time it happens at night, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat,” or “I’m angry, so I need food.” It’s more blurred than that. The day is over, things are quiet, and I’ll realize I feel unsettled or tense or just kind of worn down, but I don’t always name it in the moment. Then I end up eating, and afterward I can look back and wonder whether I was trying to calm myself down or distract myself.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there’s an emotional piece, but I don’t know how clearly I recognize it while it’s happening. It’s not like I have a neat pattern written out in my head. It’s more that the eating tends to happen when I’m not feeling quite okay, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to, during these nighttime episodes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me the most, because it doesn’t feel like just deciding to have a snack and then stopping.

Once I start, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband or there’s been that kind of strained feeling in the house, it’s like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll be standing there or sitting there, and I’m aware enough to know what’s happening, but not enough to really interrupt it. I’ll literally think, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’ll still reach for more. And then more after that.

It feels very strange to explain, because I know I have hands, I know I’m the one doing it, so it sounds like I should just be able to stop. But in the moment it feels like there’s this pull, or like stopping would require more effort than I can access right then. I’m not saying I black out or anything like that — I know what I’m doing — but I don’t feel fully in control of it. It’s more like I keep postponing stopping: “Okay, after this,” and then “just one more,” and then somehow it keeps going even though part of me wants it to be over.

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… I’m here because my eating has gotten really out of hand, and I’m embarrassed even saying that. It’s mostly at night, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the kitchen and tell myself I’m just going to have a little bit of leftovers, like something normal, and then somehow it turns into… a lot. Like, I’ll realize I’ve eaten half a tray of catered pasta, then the dessert I brought home from a client dinner, and then whatever snacks were in the pantry, all in one sitting.\n\nIt feels awful to admit, because during the day I can be very put together — I’m a marketing manager, I’m used to managing people and details and presentations — but then at night it’s like I’m standing there with containers open and wrappers around, and I suddenly see how much I’ve eaten. That’s why I came in. I don’t feel like I have a good handle on it anymore.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating at night, do you feel like you can stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I can, and I’ll even sort of make a bargain in my head, like, “Okay, Patricia, just this one plate,” or “Just finish this and then go upstairs.” But once I’ve started, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband or I’m already wound up, it feels like the part of me that makes sensible decisions just… shuts off.\n\nI’m aware of it while it’s happening, which is the worst part. I’ll be thinking, “Stop. This is enough. You’re not even enjoying it anymore.” But my hand is still opening another container or reaching back into the pantry. It’s like I’m watching myself continue, and I can’t seem to interrupt it in the moment.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these nighttime eating episodes, once you've realized what you've done?\n\nPatient: Afterward I feel… honestly, embarrassed. Deeply embarrassed. Not just like, “Oh, I ate too much,” but like I’ve done something shameful and weak, which I know sounds harsh, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nI’ll look at the containers or the wrappers and feel disgusted with myself, like, “What is wrong with you? You’re a grown woman. You’re supposed to be disciplined. You manage a team, you hold everything together, and then you’re standing in the kitchen doing this.” It feels like I’ve completely blown the version of myself I try so hard to be during the day — controlled, competent, put together.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about what I should have done instead. Like, I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or just gone upstairs and brushed my teeth and gone to bed like a normal person. I replay it in my head, almost like if I shame myself enough I’ll prevent it next time, but of course that doesn’t really help. I mostly just end up sitting there feeling awful about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty while you are eating at night, or is the guilt something that comes after you've finished?\n\nPatient: It’s more after I’ve finished, I think. I mean, there can be this little voice during it saying, “You shouldn’t be doing this,” but the real guilt — the heavy, horrible part — lands afterward, when I sort of come back to myself and see what’s there.\n\nThat’s when I feel embarrassed and disgusted with myself. I’ll look at the dishes or containers and think, “How did you let this happen again?” It feels like I’ve blown up this disciplined, capable person I spend all day trying to be. And then my mind goes straight to all the things I should have done instead — like I should have gone for a run, or done yoga, or gone upstairs before it got to that point. I just sit there feeling like I failed myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what you're eating when these nighttime episodes start?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t. I mean, at the beginning I can convince myself that I’m making a choice — like I’m just going to have a little something, or I’ll stop after one thing — but once it starts, especially if it’s after one of those tense nights with my husband, it’s like something switches off.\n\nThe part of me that normally weighs things out and says, “Okay, Patricia, enough, go upstairs,” just kind of goes offline. And I’m still there mentally, if that makes sense. I’m not unaware. I’ll be thinking, “Stop, this is enough, you don’t need more,” but my body is still moving around the kitchen, still reaching for things, still opening something else. So it doesn’t feel like I’m calmly deciding what to eat. It feels more like I’m being pulled along by it, even while another part of me is trying to slam on the brakes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating quickly during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very quickly, actually. That’s one of the things that bothers me when I think back on it.\n\nI’m usually standing at the kitchen island, not even sitting down like it’s a real meal, and I’ll just be… eating while I’m scrolling through work emails or half-looking at my phone. And it’s fast. Like, I’m barely chewing properly sometimes, or I’m already reaching for the next bite before I’ve really tasted the one I just had.\n\nThere are times where I’ll look down and the container is just empty, and I have this moment of, “Wait, when did that happen?” Not like I blacked out, but more like I wasn’t really registering it. I couldn’t tell you if it tasted good after the first few bites. I just know I kept eating, and then suddenly it was gone. That part feels awful, because it makes me feel like I wasn’t even enjoying it — I was just shoveling it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel anxious, stressed, or upset right before these nighttime eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those out, honestly. I mean… I don’t always stop and think, “I’m anxious right now,” right before it happens. Sometimes it’s only afterward that I look back and think, okay, maybe I was wound up or unsettled.\n\nThere are definitely evenings where I feel tense or kind of emotionally stirred up before I end up in the kitchen, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s always a clear feeling of anxiety or stress in the moment. Sometimes it’s more like this restless, uncomfortable feeling — like I can’t settle down, but I’m not naming it as anything. Other times I’m just tired and sort of moving through the evening on autopilot, and then suddenly I’m eating.\n\nSo… I think there’s often something going on beforehand, but I’m not sure I’ve paid close enough attention to say exactly what it is every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to control the amount of food you eat once you start eating at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. That’s probably the most upsetting part of it, because I can tell myself beforehand, “Just have a small amount,” or “Just take one thing and then go to bed,” but once I actually start, it doesn’t feel like I can manage the amount in any normal way.\n\nEspecially on nights when things have been tense with my husband, it’s like the part of me that usually makes decisions just shuts down. I’ll be standing there thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then my hand is still reaching for something else, or I’m opening another package, or going back for more even though I already know I’m going to feel terrible about it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there calmly choosing a portion and being okay with it. It feels more like once the eating starts, the stopping point disappears. I know there should be a stopping point, but I can’t seem to make myself follow it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're eating at night because you're feeling emotional, like upset or anxious?\n\nPatient: I think maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I can say that clearly. Like, I don’t always have this obvious feeling of “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.” It’s not that neat in my head.\n\nThere are nights where, looking back, I can see I probably was emotional — tense, or bothered, or just kind of unsettled — and then the eating happened. But in the actual moment, it often feels less like I’m making that connection and more like I’m just… drifting into it. I’ll be in the kitchen and then I’m eating, and only afterward I start trying to piece together what I was feeling before.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure. It might be connected to emotions sometimes, but I haven’t really been able to pin down whether that’s the reason every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, yes, that’s exactly what it feels like, and it’s hard to explain without sounding like I’m making excuses, because I know technically I’m the one eating. But in those moments, once I’ve started, it really does feel like the decision-making part of me kind of goes offline.\n\nEspecially if it’s been a tense night with my husband, or there’s been that uncomfortable feeling in the house, I’ll start eating and then it’s like I can’t get the brakes to work. I can be thinking very clearly, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need more.” And I’ll believe that. I’ll want to stop. But then I’m still reaching for something else, or taking another bite, or going back again.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I do know. That’s part of why it feels so awful. It’s like there’s this split between what I’m thinking and what I’m actually doing, and the thinking part just isn’t strong enough in that moment to make me stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating at night is usually a response to feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question, but I really don’t have a clean answer for it.\n\nSometimes I can look back afterward and think, okay, maybe I was tense, or irritated, or upset in a way I wasn’t really admitting to myself. Like there’s this background feeling that something is wrong, and then later the eating happens. But in the moment, I don’t always experience it as, “I’m emotional, so I’m going to eat.” It’s not that direct for me.\n\nA lot of the time it feels almost automatic, or like I don’t notice what led up to it until after it’s already happened. Then I’m sitting there trying to reconstruct it, like, “Was I anxious? Was I upset? Was I just tired?” And I honestly don’t always know.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, yes, but I can’t say it’s usually that, because I haven’t been able to track it clearly enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of your actions while you're eating at night?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’m physically doing it, so part of me wants to say I must be in control, because it’s my hand reaching for the food and I’m the one standing there in the kitchen. But it doesn’t feel like control the way it should feel.\n\nOnce I start, especially after one of those tense nights with my husband where I already feel kind of wound up or unsettled, it’s like something switches off. The decision-making part of me just sort of goes offline. I can be completely aware of what’s happening and still not be able to stop it.\n\nI’ll literally be thinking, “Patricia, stop. This is enough. You don’t need any more.” And I mean it when I think it. It’s not like I’m enjoying it in some carefree way and deciding to keep going. I’m thinking I should stop, I want to stop, I’m telling myself to stop — and then I’m still reaching for more. Or I’ll finish something and think, okay, that’s it, and then a minute later I’m opening something else.\n\nSo no, in those moments I don’t feel fully in control. It feels like I’m watching myself keep going even though the part of me that knows better is trying to get me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t weigh myself regularly, partly because I know it can make me feel worse, so I don’t have a clear number to give you.\n\nI’ve had moments where my clothes feel a little different, or I wonder if I’ve gained, but I don’t know if that’s an actual significant change or just me being very aware of my body and worrying about it. I haven’t been tracking it in any organized way. So I guess the most honest answer is: I’m not sure that I’ve noticed anything major recently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or self-critical while you are eating at night, as opposed to just after you've finished?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly. I mean, afterward is definitely when it becomes clearer to me — that’s when I can sit there and think, “Why did I do that?” or start replaying it in my head.\n\nDuring it, though… sometimes there’s a running commentary, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. It’s more like this uncomfortable awareness that I probably shouldn’t be doing it, or that I’m going to feel bad about it. I might think, “This isn’t a good idea,” but at the same time I’m still eating, so it’s kind of muddled.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as being self-critical while it’s happening, because it’s not always a clear, harsh thought in the moment. A lot of the sharper self-criticism seems to come after I’ve stopped and I’m more fully aware of what happened. During it, it’s more like… I know, but I’m not fully letting myself know, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat at night because you're feeling emotional?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I mean, it often feels connected to how I’m feeling, but I don’t know if I can say it’s always because I’m emotional in some direct, obvious way.\n\nSometimes by the time it happens at night, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m sad, so I’m going to eat,” or “I’m angry, so I need food.” It’s more blurred than that. The day is over, things are quiet, and I’ll realize I feel unsettled or tense or just kind of worn down, but I don’t always name it in the moment. Then I end up eating, and afterward I can look back and wonder whether I was trying to calm myself down or distract myself.\n\nSo… yes, maybe there’s an emotional piece, but I don’t know how clearly I recognize it while it’s happening. It’s not like I have a neat pattern written out in my head. It’s more that the eating tends to happen when I’m not feeling quite okay, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started, even if you want to, during these nighttime episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s actually one of the parts that scares me the most, because it doesn’t feel like just deciding to have a snack and then stopping.\n\nOnce I start, especially if it’s been a tense night with my husband or there’s been that kind of strained feeling in the house, it’s like the decision-making part of me just goes offline. I’ll literally think, “Patricia, stop, this is enough,” and then I’ll still reach for more.
